`port/build/smoke_test.ps1`:

```ps1
$ErrorActionPreference = 'Continue'
Set-Location 'H:\ghidra\septerra_core'

$p = Start-Process -FilePath '.\SR-Septerra.exe' -WorkingDirectory 'H:\ghidra\septerra_core' -PassThru
$exited = $p.WaitForExit(8000)
if (-not $exited) {
    Write-Output 'STILL_RUNNING after 8s'
    Stop-Process -Id $p.Id -Force
} else {
    Write-Output ("EXITED code=" + $p.ExitCode)
}

Write-Output '--- sessions ---'
& qwinsta 2>&1 | Out-String
Write-Output '--- recent app errors mentioning Septerra ---'
Get-WinEvent -FilterHashtable @{LogName='Application'; StartTime=(Get-Date).AddMinutes(-10)} -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
  Where-Object { $_.Message -match 'Septerra' } |
  Select-Object -First 3 TimeCreated, ProviderName, @{n='Msg';e={$_.Message.Substring(0,[Math]::Min(600,$_.Message.Length))}} |
  Format-List | Out-String

```

`port/docs/history.md`:

```md
# Publishing history

Why a 1999 PC RPG needed a publisher hunt, and when. Sources at the bottom; anything not
sourced is marked as unverified.

## Timeline

| When | What | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 1994 | Team forms at **Viacom New Media** (Chicago); ships *MTV's Beavis and Butt-Head in Virtual Stupidity* | Valkyrie history |
| 1996-97 | *Septerra Core* in development at VNM; a **vertical slice demo** is built from existing VNM backgrounds and characters and used to shop the game to publishers | community record (unverified) |
| summer 1997 | Viacom announces it is **closing the Chicago studio**. The team forms **Valkyrie Studios**, acquires the rights to the game, and goes looking for a publisher | Valkyrie history |
| Dec 1998 | **Monolith Productions acquires North American publishing rights** | IGN |
| 1999 | Released on Windows. TopWare Interactive handles European retail, Monolith North America | PCGamingWiki, Wikipedia |

The window that matters for this project: between summer 1997 and December 1998 the game had
**no publisher signed**. That is when playtest feedback was being used in pitches.

## Unverified claims

These circulate in fan discussion and video descriptions, and are recorded here rather than
asserted:

- a **$1.2 million** deal with **TopWare Interactive** in 1997;
- an offer from **Electronic Arts** that would have rebranded the game under the **Ultima**
  franchise, declined to keep creative control.

TopWare's involvement as European publisher is documented; the figure and the EA offer are not.

## Demo recordings

- "Septerra Core Vertical Slice Demo 1997" — recording of the 1997 demo on YouTube.
- Earlier 1996 pitch/prototype videos from the Viacom New Media era also survive.

Linked rather than embedded: they are someone else's recordings of unreleased material.

## Sources

- Valkyrie Studios, "A Brief History of Valkyrie Studios, Inc." — https://valkyrie-studios.com/Welcome.htm
- IGN, "Monolith Grabs Septerra Core", 1 December 1998 — https://insiderdownloads.ign.com/articles/1998/12/01/monolith-grabs-septerra-core
- PCGamingWiki, Septerra Core — https://pcgamingwiki.com/wiki/Septerra_Core:_Legacy_of_the_Creator
- Wikipedia, Septerra Core — https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Septerra_Core

```

`port/docs/pack_authoring.md`:

```md
# Authoring a sprite pack

Everything below is confirmed against real files and verified by rendering outside the game.
The goal of this document is that someone can replace one sprite and see it in game.

## What a sprite is

A sprite frame is 8-bit indexed pixels plus a palette. There is no compression and no scaling
anywhere in the engine — the blitter copies one byte to one 16-bit word, clipped only.

## File layout (AM04)

```
+0x00  'AM04'
+0x04  uint32  type            0xFFFFFFFF for images whose record name starts I, S or E
+0x08  uint32  -> A            small table
+0x10  uint32  -> B            table
+0x18  uint32  -> palettes     0x400 bytes each: 256 x 0x00BBGGRR
+0x1C  uint32  palette count
+0x20  uint32  -> D            0x18-byte frame entries
+0x28  uint32  -> E            pixel indices, one byte per pixel
+0x30  uint32  -> F            run descriptors, 8 bytes each
+0x38  uint32  -> G            row table, 8 bytes per row
+0x40  uint32  unused
```

At load the engine adds the file base to each of those eight offsets, then replaces `+0x18` with its
own array of 0x230-byte records (a pointer to the source palette followed by a 512-byte RGB565
conversion at +4). Rendering from the file only needs the 32-bit palettes.

## Structures

```
D entry (0x18)   +0x00 packed (height << 16 | width)
                 +0x10 row index into G
                 +0x14 width

G row (8)        +0 offset of this row's first run
                 +4  number of runs

F run (8)        +0 offset into E
                 +4 skip   (advance x, draw nothing)
                 +6 count  (copy this many indices)
```

Rows are drawn left to right: `x += skip`, then `count` pixels are copied straight from E.

## Reading a file

```
python3 port/tools/am04_sheet.py <file.bin> sheet.png      # every entry, 13 per row
python3 port/tools/render_am04.py <file.bin> --list        # geometry of every entry
python3 port/tools/render_am04.py <file.bin> --entry 7 --out one.png
```

Consecutive D entries are frames of the same object, so an animation shows up as a run of adjacent
cells. Entries that render blank are blank in the file — they are not a decoder bug.

## Replacing a sprite

The smallest honest replacement changes pixels without changing geometry:

1. Render the frame you want to replace and note its width and height.
2. Author a new image at the same size, indexed to 256 colours or fewer.
3. Build a new palette record (0x400 bytes, one `0x00BBGGRR` dword per entry) and new run
   descriptors that cover your image. Row runs are `(skip, count)`; a wide image is one run of
   `count = width` with `skip = 0`.
4. Append your index bytes to E, point your descriptors at them, and repoint the D entry's
   `row index` at your new rows.
5. Write the file back with all eight section offsets updated.

Keeping the dimensions identical means the game's clipping and the object's collision graphics stay
valid; changing them is a separate problem.

## What is still missing before this ships

- A writer, not just a reader: nothing yet emits a valid AM04 file with recomputed section offsets.
- Key-to-artwork naming: we know keys (`0x01001f4b` and friends) but not a human-readable index of
  which file holds which scene.
- Verification in game: the pack layer replaces whole blobs, so an edited file can be dropped in
  today — but it has not been tried end to end.

## Honest limits

- Only files loaded through `FUN_0040f000` follow this layout. Audio (`VSSF`) and character
  (`CH14`) records are documented elsewhere and are untouched by this work.
- The engine converts palettes to RGB565 on load; a 24-bit-authored palette will lose precision at
  conversion, exactly as the original art did.

## The writer exists: first pack built

`port/tools/am04_replace.py` rewrites one entry's index bytes in place, leaving the file
structurally identical — same sections, same offsets, same size, same run descriptors. That matters
because the engine loads it exactly as it loaded the original, with nothing to re-validate.

```
python3 am04_replace.py FILE --entry 6 --pattern stripes --out NEW.bin
```

With `--pattern stripes` only non-background pixels of the entry are recoloured, so the object keeps
its silhouette. `--pattern fill` replaces the whole cell, frame included.

### What was produced

Target: key `0x1001f4b`, entry 6 — the tilted disc icon, 37x37. 1,508 of 2,701 pixels rewritten in
the palette's four most saturated colours. Before/after in `pack_before_after.png`.

Verification: 2,699 bytes differ in a 710,859-byte file, all inside section E. The engine reports

```
legacy-pack: override key 0x1001f4b (710859 bytes) <- ...\pack\blobs\01001f4b.bin
```

so the modified file is genuinely the one being loaded.

### Not yet proven

The disc is an inventory icon, and inventory is not reachable from the opening cutscene, so this has
not been seen on screen. The file loads; the picture has not been watched.

## Visibility test: twelve files, none on that screen

Twelve AM04 files were repainted wholesale (every index of every entry rewritten to palette entries
254/255, which were set to magenta and lime) and loaded one at a time through the pack layer. Each
run confirmed the override fired:

```
01001f40 01001f41 01001f42 01001f43 01001f45 01001f46
01001f47 01001f48 01001f49 01001f4a 01001f4c 08001f57   -> overrides=1, magenta pixels=0
```

Screenshots were taken every 150 frames across the opening cutscene and scanned for magenta; the
baseline run confirmed zero false positives.

**None of these twelve is drawn on that screen.** That is a clean negative result across the whole
`0x01001f4x` family plus one `0x0800` file.

So the cutscene's visible UI comes from somewhere else. Candidates, in order of likelihood:

1. the `TX00` texture records (12 of them, ~600 bytes each) — texture descriptors rather than art;
2. `CH14` character records;
3. the 36x637 surface that gets blitted repeatedly, whose source has not been identified.

The last one is the most tractable: we can dump that surface and search every blob for the matching
pixel run, which identifies its key by content rather than by guessing.

## Correction: the first pack did work, and we misread the evidence

The very first in-game test - the 37x37 disc from `0x0101001f4b`, recoloured with diagonal stripes -
**was visible on screen**. The screenshot showed regular parallel cyan/blue diagonal stripes across
the disc, and that description was written down at the time as "bright light-blue/cyan diagonal
stripes reflecting across its surface, giving it a glossy, metallic appearance" - then dismissed as
the object's natural specular highlight.

It was the replacement pattern. The palette indices chosen for the stripes happened to be the file's
bluest entries, so the result looked like plausible sheen rather than an obvious debug colour.

Two process failures to avoid repeating:

1. **Do not use a colour that already occurs in the art.** A replacement pattern must be a colour
   the original cannot produce (magenta/lime on a blue object), or the change is ambiguous.
2. **A 7040x2160 capture makes a 37x37 icon ~30 pixels wide.** Vision passes over downscaled crops
   described the pattern but could not judge its scale. Crop to the icon and upscale with
   nearest-neighbour before deciding whether art changed.

Consequence: everything after this point that assumed "the pack does not reach the screen" -
the twelve-file sweep, the dark silhouettes investigation - was chasing a solved problem.

## Overlay replacement (runtime art, no file rewrite)

An alternative to rewriting an AM04 file: name a sprite (data file key + entry) and give the pack a
replacement image. The blitter hook draws it in place, at present time, so it is never painted over.

This is the path for hand-drawn or upscaled art, because it needs no re-encoding of palette, runs or
row tables - any image can be dropped in.

### Finding the target

Run `Trace Blits.bat`, play to the screen you care about, and read `blit_keys.log`:

    blit-key: key=01001F4B entry=7 frame=0 rect=16,342 89,379

`key` and `entry` identify the sprite; `rect` is x1,y1 x2,y2, so that one is 73x37 at 16,342.

**A slot is often more than one entry.** 01001F4B entry 7 is the idle inventory slot and entry 6 is
the same slot hovered - they are alternatives, not layers. Replace both, or the change appears to do
nothing whenever the cursor happens to be over it.

### Making the art

    python3 port/tools/png_scl.py to-scl myart.png myart.scl          # native size
    python3 port/tools/png_scl.py to-scl myart.png myart.scl --scale 2  # 2x, overflows its cell

- Author at the sprite's own size (`rect` width and height) for a layout-safe replacement.
- Alpha below 128, and pure black, both become transparent (the hook skips those pixels).
- `--scale 2` makes each source pixel a 2x2 block on screen. It adds no detail by itself and the
  engine still reserves the original cell, so the sprite spills over its neighbours.
- Convert back to edit: `png_scl.py to-png myart.scl myart.png`.

### Installing

Put the `.scl` in `pack/scale/` and list it in `pack/scale/scale.txt`:

    01001F4B 7 myart.scl

Then launch with `Play Scaled.bat`. Loader lines in the log confirm what was picked up:

    scale: key 01001F4B entry 7 -> 73x37 x1 (myart.scl)

```

`port/docs/pack_format.md`:

```md
# Asset pack format — draft v0.1

What the engine actually does with art, what a pack can therefore override, and the format
those overrides take. Everything marked *(evidence)* was read out of the 1.04 binary; everything
marked *(open)* is not yet established and the spec stays vague on purpose.

## 1. How art enters the engine *(evidence)*

All content comes out of the `.db` archives through one API, implemented in
`Game-DataFiles.c` on the recomp side:

| function | address | role |
|---|---|---|
| `RecordTryOpen_asm2c` | 0x445ae0 | open by key, `0xffffffff` on miss |
| `RecordGetSize_asm2c` | 0x445f30 | blob length |
| `RecordRead_asm2c` | 0x445c80 | read blob into a `malloc` buffer |
| `RecordClose_asm2c` | 0x445ec0 | release handle |

Twelve functions call `RecordRead_asm2c`. Each follows the same shape:

```c
uVar4 = RecordTryOpen_asm2c(key);
_Size = RecordGetSize_asm2c(uVar4);
buf   = malloc_asm2c(_Size);
RecordRead_asm2c(uVar4, buf, _Size);
RecordClose_asm2c(uVar4);
```

Loaded blobs carry a magic. `FUN_0040e2a0` reads one and checks `0x30305647` (`"GV00"`), then
fixes up internal offsets in place — so records are relocatable blobs with an internal pointer
table, not flat bitmaps.

## 2. The animation frame record, 0x230 bytes *(evidence)*

`StartAnimation` (0x40e900) computes the address of frame *n* as:

```c
frame = *(int *)(animRoot + 0x18) + *(ushort *)(anim + 0x190) * 0x230;
```

and `CreateAnimFrame` (`FUN_0040e6d0`) allocates and initialises that record:

```c
_Size = 0x230;                       /* no palette  */
if (hasPalette) _Size = 0x630;       /* 0x230 + 0x400 = 256 * 4 palette entries */
...
for (iVar3 = 0x8c; iVar3 != 0; ...)  /* copy 0x8C dwords = 0x230 bytes */
    *puVar5 = *puVar4;
param_1[0x8b] = hasPalette ? param_1 + 0x8c : 0;   /* +0x22C -> palette at +0x230 */
```

Two independent confirmations of 0x230: the `malloc` size, and the `0x8C` dword copy.

Known field offsets inside the record:

| offset | contents |
|---|---|
| `+0x208` | mode, `param_6` ∈ {0, 1, 2} — selects the scroll-rate formula |
| `+0x20C` | origin offset X *(zeroed on create)* |
| `+0x210` | origin offset Y *(zeroed on create)* |
| `+0x214` | origin offset Z *(zeroed on create)* |
| `+0x218` | `param_5` (duration/frame-time passed to `StartAnimation`) |
| `+0x219` | byte, feeds the X scroll-rate |
| `+0x21A` | byte, feeds the Y scroll-rate |
| `+0x21B` | byte, feeds the Z scroll-rate |
| `+0x21C` | float, X rate `((K - b21B) - off20C) / duration` |
| `+0x220` | float, Y rate |
| `+0x224` | float, Z rate |
| `+0x228` | 0 |
| `+0x22C` | pointer to the 256-entry palette, or 0 |
| `+0x230` | palette (only when `+0x22C != 0`), 256 × 4 bytes |

The bytes at `+0x219..0x21B` and the `K` constant at `_DAT_00495e48` drive per-frame scroll
rates — these are the tiling/parallax parameters, and they are why a replaced frame must keep
the original record's timing bytes intact even if its pixels change.

## 3. Is there a scaling blit? No *(evidence)*

`WhoCalls` over the GDI/DDraw imports finds exactly one renderer entry point, `FUN_00418ce0`,
calling `BitBlt`, `CreateCompatibleDC`, `SelectObject` and `DeleteDC`. `StretchBlt`,
`CreateCompatibleBitmap`, `CreateDIBitmap` and `SetDIBits` are **not imported at all**. DirectDraw
is COM, so its surface calls are indirect vtable dispatches with no symbol xrefs — which is why
the call-graph walk reported `graphics = 1`.

Consequence, and it constrains the whole format: **the shipped engine cannot scale a blit.**
A replaced frame either keeps its original pixel dimensions, or the shim must gain a scaling
blit before larger art can be used.

## 4. Pack layout

```
packs/<packname>/
  manifest.json
  blobs/<record-key>.bin           # blob-level: whole record replaced, opaque
  frames/<set>/<index>.png         # frame-level: one animation frame replaced
```

## 5. `manifest.json` — `legacy-recreated-pack/1`

```json
{
  "format": "legacy-recreated-pack/1",
  "name": "Example Pack",
  "version": "0.1.0",
  "author": "you",
  "requires": ["frame-override"],
  "frames": [
    {
      "set": "sprite-set-key",
      "index": 12,
      "file": "frames/sprite-set-key/12.png",
      "source_size": [64, 96],
      "mode": "same-size",
      "notes": "upscaled 4x then downsampled to source size"
    }
  ],
  "blobs": []
}
```

Rules the loader enforces:

- `format` must match; unknown fields ignored, unknown `requires` → pack refused, not half-loaded.
- `source_size` must equal the original frame's dimensions while `mode` is `same-size`.
- Missing `file` or a size mismatch is a pack error, never a silent fallback to a half-replaced
  frame — a visibly broken sprite is better than a mysteriously wrong one.
- Loading is all-or-nothing per frame; packs can be disabled by config without touching files.

## 6. v0 implemented — `port/patches/0002-legacy-pack-blob-override.patch`

The blob layer is real code in `Game-DataFiles.c` now, not a proposal. Three environment
variables drive it, all read once at first record open:

| variable | effect |
|---|---|
| `LEGACY_PACK_DIR` | directory to read overrides from; unset = stock behaviour |
| `LEGACY_PACK_TRACE=1` | log every record key the game opens, with size and whether it was overridden |
| `LEGACY_PACK_DUMP` | write every opened record to `<dir>/<key>.bin` — this is how you get originals to build a pack from |

Override lookup is `<dir>/blobs/<key>.bin`, key as 8 lowercase hex digits, zero-padded
(`05000001.bin`). Unpadded names are tried as a fallback. Quoted values and trailing
separators are tolerated, because `set LEGACY_PACK_DIR="D:\packs\"` is what people type.

### What is verified

- **Substitution happens.** Overriding key `0x5000001` with a 4,630-byte file produced
  `legacy-pack: override key 0x5000001 (4630 bytes) <- ...\05000001.bin` and the process kept
  running. The record came from the pack, not the archive.
- **Extraction works.** A 12-second headless run with `LEGACY_PACK_DUMP` wrote **225 records,
  56 MB**, with sizes matching the archive index (`0x2000009` → 2,502,586 bytes,
  `0x200000b` → 19,476,566 bytes).

### What is not verified

- **Visual correctness.** Everything above ran in a session with no display. Nobody has yet
  eyeballed a replaced sprite; that needs a desktop run with a real pack.
- **Key → content mapping.** The trace gives keys and sizes, not names. The dumped files are
  raw blobs; which one is which sprite is still an open question (see section 7).

### Practical loop this enables

```
LEGACY_PACK_DUMP=D:\dump   SR-Septerra.exe     # 1. extract what the game loads
                                # 2. find the blob you want (by size, by eye, by editing)
                                # 3. edit it, write D:\pack\blobs\<key>.bin
LEGACY_PACK_DIR=D:\pack    SR-Septerra.exe     # 4. run with the override, trace confirms it
```

## 7. Staged capability — what ships when

| stage | override point | needs |
|---|---|---|
| v0 | whole blob by record key | nothing but the loader — works on the recomp today |
| v1 | per animation frame | a hook at the frame-table fill (`CreateAnimFrame` / `StartAnimation`) |
| v2 | arbitrary size, filtered/scaled | a scaling blit in the shim; then internal resolution work |

v0 is deliberately first: it needs no decompiled code, only a lookup in the record reader, and
it proves the key scheme against real content.

## 8. Open questions *(open)*

- **Record key stability.** Keys arrive as integers (`RecordTryOpen_asm2c(key)`); how they are
  derived from names (`GetRecordName_`, 0x4106e0) is not established. Until it is, blob-level
  packs must ship explicit integer keys and be validated per game version.
- **Which record holds which sprite set.** Twelve loaders read records; only the graphics ones
  matter, and their mapping to visual content is unconfirmed.
- **Full 0x230 header.** Offsets above are the ones the code provably touches; the rest of the
  record is believed to hold the source surface description and is not yet decoded.
- **Palette identity.** Whether a frame's palette is authoritative per frame or shared per sprite
  set decides whether v1 packs may recolour independently.

## 9. Legal

This document specifies a **format**, not assets. Any pack, upscaled or original, is derived from
the game's art and cannot be redistributed. The project ships the loader and, eventually, the
pack-building tool; end users generate packs from their own copy.

```

`port/docs/plain_english.md`:

```md
# Plain English

For anyone who wants to know what this project is without learning to read machine code first.
No jargon without a plain sentence next to it.

## The situation

Septerra Core shipped in 1999 as a **compiled program**. Think of a finished meal: you can taste
it, but the recipe was never published. We own the dish, not the instructions.

So there are three separate things to build, and it helps to keep them apart:

1. **The Frame** — a modern wrapper that lets the original engine run on today's computers.
   This already exists; it's the recompilation by M-HT that we build and test.
2. **The Notes** — reading the compiled program and writing down what it does in a form humans
   can read. Slow, unglamorous, and the only way to truly own the game.
3. **The Art Layer** — a way to swap the pictures without touching the program at all.

Most people assume #3 needs #2 finished. It doesn't. Pictures can be replaced while the engine
stays exactly as it is.

## Words you'll keep hearing

| word | plain meaning | comparison |
|---|---|---|
| compiled program | the game as shipped — instructions the computer understands, not people | a cooked meal |
| decompilation | reading the finished program and reconstructing the recipe | tasting backwards to write the recipe |
| recompilation | translating the original instructions so a modern machine runs them | re-printing a book in a new format |
| shim | the adapter that translates old system calls into modern ones | a power adapter for foreign sockets |
| function | one job the program does | one tool in a kitchen |
| struct | a labelled form describing one thing | a character sheet with boxes |
| state machine | the game's list of modes it switches between | a theatre's scenes and act changes |
| slot | a numbered place for a character on screen (there are 13) | parking spaces |
| `.db` archive | the suitcase holding all the game's files | a suitcase |
| record key | the tag on each item in the suitcase, a number | luggage tag |
| blob | one item in the suitcase, still packed | a packed item |
| pack | a folder of replacement items that overrides the suitcase | aftermarket parts |
| oracle | the working build we compare against | a control group |

## How the pieces fit

- The **Frame** runs the original engine.
- The **pack layer** feeds that engine different pictures.
- The **Notes** slowly replace the engine itself with readable code, one function at a time.

You can ship art packs long before the engine is rewritten. That's the point of doing them
first: visible progress, no waiting.

## The path, in three stages

**Stage 1 — packs work end to end.** Weeks, not months. Pictures go in, game shows them.
What's missing: names for the entries, so you can find the picture you want without a hex number.

**Stage 2 — better pictures.** Per-animation-frame replacement, and the ability to draw art
larger than the original. This is where user-made upscales start to be worth anything.

**Stage 3 — the readable engine.** Porting the program into maintainable source code. Years of
work, and the reason to keep the Notes going all along.

## Who does what

| you | me |
|---|---|
| taste, art direction, decisions | reading machine code |
| playing and testing | writing and building the code |
| saying "that looks wrong" | keeping the notes and tools honest |
| choosing what a pack should look like | making packs possible |

## Learning by doing, not by reading

The fastest way in is one loop you can see: change one thing, run the game, look at the result.
Every stage above is built to give you that loop. If an explanation isn't leading to something
you can see, it's the wrong explanation.

```

`port/docs/renderer.md`:

```md
# The painter, located

The chain that puts a scene on screen, with addresses in the original 1.04 binary. Every claim
below is from decompiled code or runtime instrumentation, and each step was reached by following
data rather than guessing at call sites.

## The chain

```
FUN_0040b650   scene driver      walks g_levelObjs, tests each object's rect on screen
   └─ FUN_00407d00   placement    works out the destination rect, clips it to 640 x height
        └─ FUN_00404b20   blitter  reads the 0x230-byte frame record, writes the pixels
```

And underneath all of it, the screen itself:

```
FUN_00402850   lock screen    ->  DAT_004ae944 = pixel base
                                  DAT_004ae94c = base + (pitch/2) * 0x300   (a line far down)
FUN_004028e0   unlock screen
```

## How each piece was found

**Screen globals.** The wrapper's `Lock` is handed the surface; scanning the engine's `.data` for a
stored pointer to that surface produced exactly one hit, at `0x4A91A4` in the recompiled binary.
The linker map names that slot `loc_4DE56C`, i.e. original address **0x4DE56C**. Fourteen functions
read it; the writer is `FUN_00406090`.

**Lock/unlock pair.** `FUN_00402850` does the work: it fetches the surface description, stores the
pixel base in `DAT_004ae944`, and computes `DAT_004ae94c = (pitch/2)*0x300 + base`. `FUN_004028e0`
calls the matching unlock and clears both. That is the classic lock → pointer + stride pattern, and
it explains the lock counts measured at runtime.

**Scene driver.** Of the 40 functions reading the pixel base, `FUN_0040b650` (353 lines) is the one
that walks `g_levelObjs`, tests `PtInRect` for visibility, and issues draws per object.

**Placement.** `FUN_00407d00` computes source and destination, clips with the literal `0x280` (640)
and `DAT_004d4ea8` (screen height), then calls the blitter.

**Blitter.** `FUN_00404b20`, 163 lines. It performs the frame-record lookup we predicted from
`StartAnimation`:

```c
local_2c = *(ushort *)(param_3 + 2) * 0x230 + *(int *)*param_1;
```

Index × 0x230 plus a base — the same stride established from the animation side. It then walks
scanlines with `ushort *` pointers (16-bit colour, matching the RGB565 surfaces measured at
runtime).

## Inside the blitter

```c
*dst = *(uint16 *)(record + 4 + *src * 2);
```

One byte in, one 16-bit word out: the byte is an index into a 256-entry table of 16-bit colours at
`record + 4`. The copy advances source by one byte and destination by one word per pixel, with
clipping only — **no step, no factor, no second sample anywhere in the routine**.

That settles the resolution question: **no scaling exists in this engine in any form.** The 2.00x
window magnification measured earlier is the whole of it.

Rows are not flat bitmaps. Each row is a sequence of `(skip, count)` runs walked from a descriptor
array, and the code handles mirrored drawing with `iVar7 = +/-1`.

*Correction:* an earlier version of this note said "the art is RGB565, not palettized". That was
measured on the composited screen surface. Source art is 8-bit indexed; the screen is RGB565. Both
are true of different ends of the same operation.

## The AM04 file format

The loader `FUN_0040f000` opens a blob by key, reads it whole, checks the magic `AM04`
(`0x34304d41`), then adds the blob base to **eight stored offsets**, turning them into pointers:

```
+0x00  char[4]  'AM04'          (overwritten with the key at load)
+0x04  uint32   type            (forced to 0xFFFFFFFF for I/S/E names)
+0x08  uint32   -> section A
+0x10  uint32   -> section B
+0x18  uint32   -> palette array        (0x400 bytes per frame)
+0x1C  uint32   frame count
+0x20  uint32   -> section D
+0x28  uint32   -> section E   pixel indices
+0x30  uint32   -> section F   run descriptors
+0x38  uint32   -> section G   run jump table
+0x40  uint32   -> section H   (unused in the files checked)
```

Verified against the live engine: for key `0x1001f41` the loaded copy and the file differ in
**39 bytes out of 8,192**, and every one is accounted for (magic → key, type → 0xFFFFFFFF, seven
relocations, and `+0x18` replaced with the engine's converted array). Everything else is identical.

### Record size

`(next section - palette array) / frame count = 0x400` exactly, on a 7-frame and a 16-frame file.
0x400 = 256 entries of 4 bytes, stored as `0x00BBGGRR`. Decoded as 32-bit colour the entries run
smoothly (0.80–0.99 by frame) with 256 distinct values: these are palettes.

**So keys like `0x1001f41` are palette banks, not pictures.** Seven palettes, probably lighting or
time-of-day variants for one scene. An earlier attempt to find palettes "inside frame records" read
noise because there are no pixel records in those files at all.

### The runtime conversion

The engine allocates its own array and rewrites `+0x18` to point at it. That array walks at
**0x230 per frame** — the same stride the blitter uses — and each record is:

```
record+0x000  pointer to the file's 32-bit palette
record+0x004  512-byte RGB565 palette (256 entries)   <- what the blitter reads
record+0x204  header: mode, origin, duration, motion
```

The chain closes: 32-bit palettes in the file, converted to 16-bit at load, read at +4 by the
blitter, with 0x230 as the stride everywhere.

## Section map across three files

| section | 01001f41 | 01001f4b | 01000fa2 | contents |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A (+0x08) | 760 | 2,432 | 1,900 | small table |
| B (+0x10) | 520 | 1,352 | 17,420 | table |
| palettes (+0x18) | 7,168 | 12,288 | 16,384 | 7 / 12 / 16 palettes |
| D (+0x20) | 480 | 1,248 | 4,944 | small table |
| **E (+0x28)** | **110,065** | **636,131** | **766,636** | **8-bit pixel indices** |
| F (+0x30) | 10,184 | 32,624 | 289,096 | run descriptors |
| G (+0x38) | 6,672 | 24,712 | 107,568 | run jump table |
| H (+0x40) | 0 | 0 | 0 | unused here |

Section E spans 0–255 with 2.8–5.7 bits/byte entropy: 8-bit artwork.

### Rendering attempt

Feeding section E to a flat 640-wide bitmap **shears but shows real content** — a regular grid,
digit strings, star-shaped icons, silhouettes. Not noise, and not the wrong section: rows are run
described (`skip, count`), so a flat read misaligns them. Transcribing the blitter's run walk
offline is the remaining step, and needs no new discovery.

## Open questions

- The exact run-walk semantics (descriptor indexing through sections F and G), needed for a clean
  offline render.
- What `0x300` in the stride computation represents — probably room below the visible frame.
- Which keys are sprite sheets versus palette banks versus scene compositions.

## Next steps, in order

1. Transcribe the blitter's run walk into `port/tools/` and render section E cleanly.
2. Use the result to identify which key holds which artwork.
3. Then a pack can substitute pixels, which is the first real art pack.

## Instrumentation available

Runtime switches in the wrapper (inert unless set):

| variable | what it does |
|---|---|
| `LEGACY_PACK_DIR` / `_TRACE` / `_DUMP` | blob override and extraction |
| `LEGACY_BLIT_LOG` | log every blit with source and destination rects |
| `LEGACY_SHOT_DIR` / `_SHOT_EVERY` | screenshot every Nth presented frame |
| `LEGACY_INPUT` | scripted clicks and keys, `t,action,x,y;...` |
| `LEGACY_DUMP_SURFACES` | write every blitted surface to BMP |
| `LEGACY_ART` | replace a blitted surface with a BMP |
| `LEGACY_FIND_GLOBAL` | locate the engine's screen-surface pointer |
| `LEGACY_FIND_IMAGE` | enumerate image structs in `.data` by shape |
| `LEGACY_IMAGE_DIR` | dump a loaded image struct and its sections |
| `LEGACY_CACHE_DUMP` | dump the engine's image cache table |
| `LEGACY_CALLERS` / `LEGACY_BT` | report call sites reaching the wrapper |

## Where the offline render stands

Not done, and the reason is specific rather than mysterious. The run-walk is transcribed from the
blitter, but it needs `param_1[4]` — the pixel pool, taken from image field `+0x40` — and **that
field is zero for every image loaded in the headless runs**: all twelve are the `0x01001f4x`
palette-bank family.

Two candidate explanations, both testable:

1. the pixel pool is built later, during scene loading, and the runs never got that far;
2. the scripted click did not actually reach gameplay this time.

Either way the next move is the same and cheap: confirm visually that the game is in a scene, then
dump the live image struct at that moment and look for a non-zero `+0x40`. Until that field is
non-zero, no offline render is possible — the indices have no base to index from.

## Gameplay confirmed, and what that rules out

The scripted click does reach the game: a screenshot at frame 24,600 shows the opening cutscene —
throne room, character portrait, dialogue box reading *"Doskias: It is not heresy. The artifacts are
meant to unlock the secrets of the Core..."*.

So the scene renderer is running with real artwork on screen, while the image structs visible in
`.data` still report `+0x40 = 0` and still belong to the `0x01001f4x` family only.

That means the scene does **not** draw from those image structs. The blitter's five-pointer input
must come from a different structure — most likely the per-object animation records (0x1C8 `Obj`,
0x230 frame records) rather than the image cache.

**Consequence:** hunting image keys was the wrong thread for scene art. The palette banks are a
real finding, but they are not what paints a scene.

## Offline sprite render: working

The five pointers passed to the blitter were one slot off in my earlier reading. `FUN_00409050`
assembles them on the stack, so `&local_18` hands over six consecutive dwords:

```
param_1[0] = &image[0x18]   -> *param_1 = palette records (0x230 stride)
param_1[1] = image[0x20]    -> section D: 0x18-byte entries
param_1[2] = image[0x38]    -> section G: row table, 8 bytes per row
param_1[3] = image[0x30]    -> section F: run descriptors, 8 bytes per run
param_1[4] = image[0x28]    -> section E: pixel indices
```

**That is the whole mystery of the zeroed `+0x40`**: the pixel pool is section E, which is inside
the file. The `+0x40` field is simply unused, and I had been reading the struct one field late.

Section layouts, confirmed by inspection:

```
D entry  (0x18 bytes): +0x10 row index, +0x14 width
G row    (8 bytes):    +0 count-run index, +4 runs in this row
F run    (8 bytes):    +0 offset into E, +4 skip, +6 count
```

`port/tools/render_am04.py` implements this and renders entries from a real file. First runs on
`01001f4b.bin`: 43x43 and 73x43 sprites appear, and they are **recognisable game art** — inventory
item icons (equipment, a chest harness, a circular disc), each inside its selection frame. Rendered
entirely outside the game, from the file on disk.

Not perfect yet: entries 0–2 look like near-duplicate frames of the same item, which suggests the
D-entry index is doing something more subtle than "one entry = one sprite" (probably animation
frames of a single item, or a per-frame table). But the pixels, palettes, runs and row addresses are
all correct — the image is the artwork, not noise.

## What this means for art packs

A pack can now be authored against a format we can read, write and verify offline: 256-colour
palette plus run-described index data. The remaining work is packaging, not discovery.

## Blitter map: which image is drawn where

`LEGACY_BLIT_HOOK=1` installs a detour at the sprite blitter and logs every distinct draw:

```
blit-key: key=08001F58 entry=10 frame=11 rect=233,198 414x376
blit-key: key=01001F40 entry=0  frame=0  rect=0,0 28x28
```

How the key is recovered: `FUN_00409050` builds the blitter's five pointers on the stack from
`&local_18`, so `p1[0]` is `&image[0x18]`. The image base is therefore `p1[0] - 0x18`, and the
loader stamps the record key at the image's offset `0` (see the live-vs-file diff for `0x1001f41`,
where `AM04` in the file becomes the key in memory). `entry` is `*(u16*)(param_3+4)` and `frame` is
`*(u16*)(param_3+2)`.

This turns "guess which file holds this art" into a lookup: play to the screen you care about, close
the game, read `blit_keys.log`. The `Trace Blits.bat` launcher does exactly that.

First map, from the title/menu sequence:

| Key | Entries drawn | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `01001F40` | 0-15, each once, stacked rects | startup sequence, 24-31 px wide |
| `08001F58` | 0-12 cycling | scene/panel art, rects up to 594x237 |
| `08001F57` | 20 | side panel at 21,418 (161x461) |

Note how much of the "scene" is one key drawn repeatedly at different entries - which is exactly the
per-frame behaviour that made file-level guessing fail.

### First full map, from a real play session

`Trace Blits.bat`, inventory opened, 56,371 logged draws, deduplicated by key:

| Key | Draws | Role |
|---|---|---|
| `08001F58` | 14,950 | scene / panel art, cycles entries per frame |
| `01001F4B` | 9,954 | inventory + HUD panels |
| `08001F4D` | 7,964 | never examined before this map |
| `01001F47` | 7,956 | UI |
| `01001F43` | 6,723 | UI |
| `01001F41` | 4,819 | palette-bank / UI |
| `01001F45` | 2,159 | UI |
| `08001F57` | 1,324 | side panel (161x461) |
| `01001F4C` | 336 | small icons |
| `01001F42` | 168 | UI |
| `01001F40` | 18 | startup sequence |

`01001F4B` by entry: `0`, `1`, `5`, `7`, `9`, `20` are drawn ~2,000 times each at large clip windows
(panel furniture), while **entry `6` is drawn 94 times with clip bounds `16,342 -> 89,379`** - a
37-pixel-tall strip in the HUD area. That is the disc icon.

So the very first replacement did target the correct entry. The map confirms it independently of the
screenshot argument, which is the point: from here, identification is a lookup, not a guess.

## Scaled replacement art (the hi-res mechanism)

The blitter copies 1 byte to 1 word, so sharper art is not a file-format problem - it is a drawing
problem. The hook now stamps pre-scaled art over the original draw, at the same origin.

Format (`*.scl`, little endian):

```
0x00  "SCL1"
0x04  u16 width        (already scaled)
0x06  u16 height       (already scaled)
0x08  u16 scale factor (2)
0x0A  width*height u16 RGB565 pixels
```

Manifest `pack/scale/scale.txt`, one line each: `KEY ENTRY FILE` (key hex, entry decimal).

```
01001F4B 6 01001F4B_6.scl
```

Engine side: `LegacyScaleLoad()` reads the manifest when the hook installs, `LegacyScaleDraw()`
writes the scaled pixels at

```
dest = dest_base + (src_x * 640 + src_y) * 2
```

where `src_x`/`src_y` come from `param_3 + 0x0C` and `+ 0x06`. Stride 640 is the 640x480 backbuffer
assumption; if art lands offset, that constant and the origin are the two things to adjust.

Pixels equal to zero are treated as transparent, which is correct for this art but will be wrong for
any sprite that legitimately uses palette index 0 as a colour.

Verified so far: manifest loads (`scale: key 01001F4B entry 6 -> 146x74 x2`), game runs 10 s without
crashing. Visual confirmation on the inventory screen is the outstanding step.

```

`port/docs/resolution_findings.md`:

```md
# Can this game draw at a higher resolution?

Short answer: **not today.** The engine asks for 640×480 in its own code, and everything it paints
is composed at that size. The window can be bigger, but the picture handed to it is already finished.

## Evidence

**1. The game requests 640×480 explicitly.** In `WinMain_` (`0x412b80`, line ~238 of the decompiled
output):

```c
FUN_00446a20(DAT_004d55a8, 0x280, 0x1e0, (undefined *)0x0);
```

`0x280` = 640, `0x1e0` = 480. Those are literals in the game's own code, not values read from
`Septerra.cfg`. `FUN_00446a20` is the display-mode call chain (DirectDraw `SetDisplayMode`).

**2. The window size is a separate thing.** In the wrapper (`WinApi-ddraw.c`), the window is created
at `Display_Width`/`Display_Height` (line 878), while the renderer is given the *game's* requested
size as its logical size (`SDL_RenderSetLogicalSize(Renderer, dwWidth, dwHeight)`). Presentation
then does `SDL_RenderCopy(..., NULL, NULL)` — the finished 640×480 frame stretched to the window.

**3. Consequence.** With `Display_Width = 1280`, you get a clean 2× enlargement of a 640×480 picture.
Every original pixel becomes a perfect 2×2 block. It is sharp, and it contains exactly the same
information as before: no extra detail exists to show.

## What this means for art packs

| goal | possible today | why |
|---|---|---|
| replace a picture with one of the same size | **yes** | the engine never knows the difference |
| fix colours, palettes, ugly compression | **yes** | same reason |
| supply art at 1280×960 for a 640×480 game | no, not usefully | it is downsampled to the frame before you see it |
| genuinely more visible detail | no | requires the engine to compose at a larger size |

So "hi-res texture pack" in the usual sense is gated, not forbidden. The art half of the work is
still real and still worth doing — it just cannot exceed the frame the engine draws into.

## The one question that decides whether hi-res is cheap or expensive

Sprites have to get onto that 640×480 frame somehow, and there are two possibilities:

- **Through the wrapper's blit function** (`IDirectDrawSurface_Blt`, which the wrapper implements).
  If so, the wrapper sees every sprite draw with its source and destination rectangles — and could
  scale those rectangles into a larger frame without touching the game's code at all.
- **By writing pixels straight into a locked surface.** Then the wrapper sees a memory buffer and
  raw bytes, with no idea where one sprite ends and the next begins. Scaling would require the
  game's drawing code to be understood and replaced.

The wrapper implements both `Blt` and `Lock`, so both paths are live. Which one the engine actually
uses per sprite is not yet established.

**How to settle it (cheap):** log every blit for one minute of real gameplay, with source and
destination rectangles. Hundreds of small blits with sub-640 rectangles means the wrapper sees the
sprite work, and a wrapper-side 2× renderer is plausible. Almost no blits means direct pixel writes,
and hi-res needs the draw path ported first.

## What this changes about the port

It shrinks the gate. Hi-res does not need all ~800 unnamed functions. It needs the **draw and
composite path** — the code that decides where each sprite goes and paints it — which is a subset
in the range of 30–80 functions, plus the wrapper side. That is a months-scale project, not a
years-scale one, and it is the piece worth attacking deliberately rather than the whole engine in
alphabetical order.

## Result (measured, headless menu run)

Instrumented `IDirectDrawSurface_Blt` and `IDirectDrawSurface_Flip` with an env-var log
(`LEGACY_BLIT_LOG`) and ran 12 seconds with no display. 10,127 presents, and the sprite work
**goes through the wrapper**:

```
blit dst=640x480 dstrect=(0,0,36,28) src=36x637 srcrect=(0,0,36,28)
blit dst=640x480 dstrect=(10,10,46,38) src=36x637 srcrect=(0,0,36,28)
frame 2 blits=3 locks=4
```

- Destination surfaces are **640×480**, as predicted.
- Blits carry **explicit source and destination rectangles** — the wrapper sees each sprite's
  position and size individually.
- Locks per frame are low (1–4), so composition is not done by painting pixels directly; the
  engine asks the wrapper to move pictures around.

**Consequence:** a wrapper-side higher-resolution renderer is architecturally plausible —
the wrapper is handed each sprite draw with coordinates it can scale. It is not a small change
(the destination is the engine's own 640×480 buffer, and anything drawn by other means has to be
handled separately), but it does not require porting the game's drawing code first.

**Caveat:** this is the menu, headless, at an unthrottled frame rate. Ratios matter, absolute
numbers do not. In-game counts need a desktop run.

## Instrumentation

`LEGACY_BLIT_LOG=<path>` writes one line per present (`frame <n> blits=<n> locks=<n>`) plus up to
300 individual blit records. Carried as `port/patches/0003-legacy-blit-log.patch`.

## Correction: in-scene drawing does not go through `Blt`

The menu measurement above only describes the **menu**. Driving the game into an actual scene
(scripted click on New Game, then watching a dialogue in the Doskias chamber) gives a different
picture:

| state | blits/frame | locks/frame |
|---|---|---|
| main menu | up to 98 | 1–4 |
| in-engine dialogue scene | **0** | **7** |

Zero blits, seven locks, sustained over 6,000 frames. The scene — pre-rendered background,
character sprites, portrait, text box — is painted by **writing pixels directly into a locked
surface**. The wrapper is handed a memory buffer, not a list of sprite draws.

So the earlier conclusion ("a wrapper-side scaled renderer is architecturally plausible") is
**wrong for gameplay**. It holds only for menus and UI. Scaling individual sprites would require
the engine's own drawing code, which is the part that has to be understood and replaced.

## What that leaves as the realistic options

1. **Post-process upscaling of the finished 640×480 frame** — cheap, wrapper-only, no new art.
   It cannot add detail, but for pixel art a good upscaler visibly improves the result. This is
   what most "HD" pixel-art re-releases actually do.
2. **Port the engine's drawing path** — the only route to genuinely higher-resolution rendering
   with sharper art. Larger than the 30–80 functions guessed earlier, because the software
   renderer *is* the per-frame bulk of the game.

Option 1 is worth having regardless; option 2 is the real project.

## Harness (why none of this needs a human at the keyboard)

Two additions make the game drivable and observable in a windowless session:

- `LEGACY_SHOT_DIR` + `LEGACY_SHOT_EVERY` — save a BMP of the rendered frame every N presents
  (`LegacyScreenshot`, `WinApi-ddraw.c`).
- `LEGACY_INPUT="6000,click,100,440;9000,key,13"` — replay clicks and keys at millisecond offsets
  (`LegacyInputPump` + `Patch_PushMouseClick`, `Game-Patch.c`). Coordinates are in the game's
  640×480 logical space; SDL's logical-size handling converts real mouse input the same way.

Together these reached the Doskias dialogue scene from a cold start with no display attached.
That is the foundation for automated visual checks of art packs later.

## Post-process upscaler: built, measured, redundant

An EPX/Scale2x filter was implemented in the wrapper (`LegacyUpscaleEPX2`, `LEGACY_UPSCALE=2x`)
and instrumented. Then the diagnostic line answered a question we had been guessing at:

```
legacy-upscale: diag surface=1280x960 bpp=2 | texture=1280x960 fmt=SDL_PIXELFORMAT_RGB565 factor=2
```

**The backbuffer is already 1280×960**, i.e. window resolution, not 640×480. The wrapper hands
the engine a surface at display size, so whatever composite we receive is already at the target
resolution. Doubling it again produced doubled/black scanlines — a correct filter applied to the
wrong stage.

Consequence: a post-process upscaler is not the missing piece. The filter is kept but
dimension-guarded so it only runs when a genuine 2× relationship exists; today it is inert.

## The question this exposes, and why it matters

If the engine composes onto a 1280×960 surface, one of two things is true:

- **It scales its art up** (from 640-wide source art) — a magnifying glass, same detail, larger.
- **It draws art 1:1 on a larger canvas** — more of the world visible, and the "extended view"
  the owner worried about already exists at any window size above 640×480.

The two look similar in a screenshot but mean opposite things for art packs. The test is cheap:
capture the same scene at 640×480 and at 1280×960, then compare how many pixels a character
occupies. Twice as many pixels everywhere = scaling. Same sprite size, more visible surroundings
= extended view.

That single comparison decides what a hi-res pack can even deliver, so it comes before any more
filter work.

## Settled: window size is pure magnification, exactly 2.00×

Same static screen — the main menu — captured at 640×480 and at 1280×960, then the bright-pixel
bounding box measured on both (no eyeballing):

| capture | bbox | width | height | origin |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 640×480 | (11,11)–(593,458) | 582 | 447 | 11,11 |
| 1280×960 | (22,22)–(1187,916) | 1165 | 894 | 22,22 |

Ratios: width **2.00**, height **2.00**, origin **2.00**. Every element sits at exactly twice the
position and twice the size. Nothing is revealed at the edges.

**Answer: there is no extended view.** Enlarging the window magnifies the same 640×480
composition; it never shows more of the world. The owner's concern about a wider field of view
constituting an unfair advantage does not apply — the capability does not exist.

## The consequence worth testing next

If the engine scales its drawing to fill the surface (which the 2.00× factor proves it does), then
it must be *sampling* art scaled rather than pixel-doubling it — and art supplied at twice the
resolution could survive at native detail. That would make high-resolution packs viable on the
current build, without porting anything.

Test: substitute one sprite record with a 2× version of itself, run at 1280×960, and compare.

That single experiment decides whether the overhaul needs the port at all.

## Pipeline settled (per-copy instrumentation)

The single-shot diagnostic was misleading me; a periodic log (every 400th copy) is unambiguous, at
a 1280×960 window:

```
copy#1    surface=1280x960 bpp=2 | texture=1280x960 factor=2
copy#401  surface=1280x960 bpp=2 | texture=1280x960 factor=2
```

The presented backbuffer **is** the window size; the texture matches it; every frame is composed at
1280×960. The `dst=640x480` lines in the earlier blit log were the engine's own offscreen canvases,
not the presentation target. Two different surfaces, which is what made the two measurements look
contradictory.

Combined with the exactly-2.00× menu geometry, the model is: **the engine composes at output
resolution and multiplies every drawing operation by a global factor** (window size ÷ 640×480).

Consequence for the hypothesis in the previous section: supplying art at twice the resolution would
most likely be drawn at twice the size again, not at higher detail — the scale factor comes from the
output size, not from the asset. That makes "hi-res packs work today" unlikely, and it must not be
claimed until a real replacement has been tried.

## What actually blocks all art work

Not resolution — **format**. The dumped records are a family of containers:

| magic | count | looks like |
|---|---|---|
| `VSSF` | 155 | audio |
| `AM04` | 40 | animation/sprite data, 47 KB–1.2 MB, header 0x48 with offset tables |
| `TX00` | 12 | texture descriptor tables, ~600 B |
| `CH14` | 12 | character records |
| `GV00`, `IL00` | 1 each | globals, level index |

Until the `AM04` sprite container is decoded — dimensions, palette, pixel layout — no pack can
replace a single picture, at any resolution. That is the gate, and it comes before every other art
task.

## First artwork extracted (surface dump)

Rather than reverse-engineer `AM04` blind, the wrapper now dumps the source surface of every
distinct blit: `LEGACY_DUMP_SURFACES=<dir>` writes `surf_<w>x<h>_<n>.bmp`, capped at 200.

First run (title screen + a click into the game) produced three:

```
640x480 bpp=2   617 KB   the complete title/splash artwork, with publisher logos
36x637  bpp=2    67 KB   a narrow UI strip
640x480 bpp=2   617 KB   a second full-screen layer
```

Findings:

- Surface pixel format is **RGB565, 16 bpp** — not palettized, contrary to the assumption carried
  from the 1999-era art discussion. Any replacement artwork must arrive in that format or be
  converted to it.
- Full-screen art is recoverable **as an image**, complete and uncut. This is the first time the
  project has produced artwork rather than numbers, and it is exactly the kind of asset a pack
  would override.
- In-scene sprites are *not* captured by this route, because they are painted by direct pixel
  writes rather than blitted (see the zero-blits measurement earlier).

So there are two distinct asset paths, and they need different treatment:

| asset kind | how it reaches the screen | pack approach |
|---|---|---|
| blitted layers (splash, UI strips, menu art) | `Blt` with a source surface | interceptable at blit time |
| in-scene sprites | painted pixel by pixel by engine code | needs `AM04`/engine understanding |

The `AM04` decode is still required for sprite work, but it is no longer the only route to
replacing artwork.

## Art override tested: the shortcut does not exist

`LEGACY_ART=<bmp>` was built to replace the contents of any blitted surface matching an image's
size, as a proof that packs could work at the wrapper level. A deliberately altered 640×480 image
(hue-rotated, flipped, magenta bars) was supplied. The log confirmed the swap:

```
legacy-art: loaded H:\ghidra\work\art_override.bmp (640x480)
legacy-art: replaced surface 640x480
```

The screen did not change. Counting blits over the title screen explains why:

| measurement | value |
|---|---|
| blits per frame at the title | 1 |
| blits of the 36×637 UI strip (12 s total) | 10,537 |
| blits of a 640×480 surface (12 s total) | **1** |
| locks per frame | 3 |

The single 640×480 blit happens once at startup; the strip is the only thing blitted repeatedly.
The score, the logo, the menu — all of it is painted by **direct pixel writes** through three locks
per frame.

**Conclusion: there is no wrapper-level art replacement for anything visible.** Only narrow UI
strips pass through `Blt`. Every substantial image reaches the screen through the engine's own
software painter, which means:

- the blob-level pack layer is still the correct insertion point;
- making it work for real art requires reading the code that paints, i.e. `AM04` plus the painter
  itself;
- the "two asset paths" table earlier was too optimistic — it is one important path and a few
  UI strips.

## Locating the painter: what worked, what didn't

The goal was to find the engine function that writes those 3–7 locks' worth of pixels per frame.
Two cheap approaches were tried and both failed honestly:

**Vtable-offset byte scan.** `IDirectDrawSurface::Lock` sits at vtable offset 0x64, so the call
should compile to `call [reg+0x64]`. A scan of the whole original `.text` for every such encoding
(disp8 and disp32, every register) found **zero** occurrences. The engine does not reach DirectDraw
that way.

**Return-address trace.** Instrumenting the shim to log `_ReturnAddress()` produced three sites:

```
blt  from 0044AFA6
lock from 0044B2E3
flip from 0044B094
```

Resolved against a linker map (`/MAP` added to the build) these are the shim's own generated
thunks (`IDirectDrawSurface_Lock_asm2c +0x23`), one level short of the engine. A stack walk was
attempted to get one level further and returned garbage — these builds have no frame pointers.

**Direct-call scan of the recompiled binary.** Searching for `E8` calls targeting each thunk found
**zero direct callers**, which means the engine reaches them indirectly through stored function
pointers.

## What exists now that didn't before

- `/MAP` in the build, giving `SR-Septerra.map`: 12,399 symbols with their addresses, including the
  original names (`GetRecordName_ 0x410400`, `WinMain_ 0x412960`). That is a working bridge from a
  runtime address to a named function.
- `LEGACY_CALLERS=1` and `LEGACY_BT=1` instrumentation in the wrapper.

## Where this leaves the painter hunt

The remaining route is data flow rather than call matching: find the display-surface object the
engine keeps, follow what it does with the pointer returned by Lock, and look for the stride
arithmetic that writes pixels. That is a Ghidra session, not a wrapper one — and it is the honest
next step, with no shortcut left to try first.

**Correction (blitter read, later):** sprite *source* art is **8-bit palettized** with a per-frame
256-entry RGB565 palette; the RGB565 finding refers to the composited screen surface. See
`renderer.md`.


```

`port/docs/scaling.md`:

```md
# Scaling: what was tried and what it proved

The goal of this thread was to make the game look better on a 1920x1080 desktop without authoring
art. Three approaches were tested.

## 1. Window magnification (works, and is all we have today)

The engine renders 640x480 into a window sized by `Septerra.cfg`:

```
Display_Mode = window
Display_Width = 1280
Display_Height = 960
```

2x is the largest exact integer multiple that fits a 1080p desktop; 3x (1920x1440) does not fit
vertically. The picture is the same 640x480 frame, magnified - no detail is added.

## 2. EPX2 upscaling (built, and broken for this engine)

`LEGACY_UPSCALE=epx` was implemented in the shim and wired into the present path. It does not work,
and the reason is structural:

```
legacy-upscale: EPX 2x enabled
legacy-upscale: copy#1 surface=1280x960 bpp=2 | texture=1280x960 factor=2
```

The shim sets `SDL_RenderSetLogicalSize(640*2, 480*2)`. The engine asks the renderer for its output
size and then **creates its backbuffer at that size** - 1280x960 instead of 640x480. Two
consequences:

1. the engine draws its 640x480 picture into a double-size canvas, so the image occupies a quarter
   of the window ("tiny");
2. the EPX guard (`surface == 640x480`) can never fire, because the surface is already 1280x960.

So the filter was never applied. Any real implementation has to keep the backbuffer at 640x480
while presenting at 1280x960, which means decoupling the two sizes in the shim.

## 3. The game's own `Scaling` config key (inconclusive)

`Game-Config.c` recognises `Scaling = basic` (bilinear) and `Scaling = basicnb` (nearest). Two runs
with each variant produced **pixel-identical** frames (0 differing pixels), so either the key is
reset before use or it has no effect at an exact 2x scale. Not resolved; lower priority than it
looks, because it is a filter, not resolution.

## What is left

Filtering can only smooth what is already there. Detail requires drawing art **larger than its
original pixel grid**, which means the blitter has to scale an override image instead of copying
1 byte to 1 word. That is a change in the renderer, not in a launcher.

### Status: scaled replacement art works

Verified on screen: the inventory disc slot (01001F4B entries 6 and 7) is drawn from a 2x
replacement at 146x74 in a 73x37 cell, in both the idle and hovered states.

Two findings that cost several failed rounds and are worth not repeating:

1. **A sprite slot can be several entries, used as alternatives.** 01001F4B entry 7 draws while the
   slot is idle, entry 6 while it is hovered. Replacing one leaves the other on screen, so the change
   looks like it did nothing. Always check the draw counts per entry in a trace before concluding.

2. **Colour-coded markers beat art for identification.** Replacing a 37-pixel icon with an upscaled
   copy of itself is nearly invisible in a screenshot; a solid block of a known colour is not.
   Marking several candidate entries in different colours in one run identifies all of them at once.

Draw order: overlay at blit time is not enough - later draws in the same frame paint over it. The hook
queues the request and paints during CopyBackbufferSurfaceToTexture, so the replacement is the last
thing written each frame.

Limit: the engine positions every element by its original size, so a doubled element spills out of
its cell. That is a layout constraint, not a hook bug.

### Whole-frame 2x (LEGACY_WINDOW)

`LEGACY_WINDOW=1280x960` makes the window 1280x960 while the engine keeps rendering 640x480.
SDL_RenderCopy stretches the finished frame, so every element scales together and the layout stays
intact. Nearest sampling by default (crisp pixels); `Scaling=linear` in the ini smooths it.

This is the safe "it runs bigger" mode. It adds no detail - it is the same 640x480 image, larger.
True added detail still needs either per-element replacement art with a layout-aware engine, or the
native port.

Do not use LEGACY_UPSCALE=2x for this: it builds the backbuffer at 1280x960 as well, so the engine
draws 640x480 content into the top-left quarter and the EPX filter never matches. That path is dead.

### Verification pitfalls (cost hours, worth remembering)

**Testing a window-size change over RDP is meaningless.** An RDP session reports its own desktop
size to Windows, so every window is clamped to it. A 640x480 window, a 1280x960 window and a
1920x1440 window all look identical on a phone-sized remote session. Test display changes at the
physical desktop, or check the resolution first: `Get-CimInstance Win32_VideoController`.

The same applies to a headless host: the virtual display reports 1024x768, so window sizes above
that are clamped there too and screenshots of the desktop come back black.

**Practical ceiling on a 1080p screen is 2x.** 1920x1440 is taller than 1080 and gets cut off at the
bottom edge, so 3x is only usable on a taller display. Septerra.cfg already pins the game to
1280x960, which is why LEGACY_WINDOW=1280x960 looks identical to a normal launch: normal *is* 2x.

```

`port/ghidra/Annotate.java`:

```java
import ghidra.app.script.GhidraScript;
import ghidra.program.model.address.Address;
import ghidra.program.model.data.DataUtilities;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.Data;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.Function;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.FunctionManager;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.Instruction;
import ghidra.program.model.mem.Memory;
import ghidra.program.model.symbol.Reference;

import java.io.BufferedReader;
import java.io.File;
import java.io.FileInputStream;
import java.io.InputStreamReader;
import java.io.PrintWriter;
import java.util.*;

/**
 * For the biggest internal ("pure engine") functions, pull the evidence you need to name them:
 * referenced string literals and named callees.
 *
 * Reads work/ghidra-out/calltree_nodes.csv, annotates the top N functions with no boundary
 * subsystem, writes work/ghidra-out/annotation.csv and prints a compact summary.
 *
 * Usage: -postScript Annotate.java [maxFunctions] [minBytes]
 */
public class Annotate extends GhidraScript {

    private static final String OUT = "H:/ghidra/work/ghidra-out/";

    private String stringAt(Address a) {
        try {
            if (!currentProgram.getMemory().contains(a)) return null;
            ghidra.program.model.mem.MemoryBlock blk = currentProgram.getMemory().getBlock(a);
            if (blk == null || !blk.isInitialized()) return null;
            if (blk.getName().toLowerCase().contains("text")) return null;   // code
            byte[] buf = new byte[96];
            int n = currentProgram.getMemory().getBytes(a, buf);
            if (n < 4) return null;
            StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
            for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
                byte c = buf[i];
                if (c == 0) break;
                if (c < 32 || c > 126) return null;      // not a plain C string
                sb.append((char) c);
            }
            if (sb.length() < 4) return null;
            String v = sb.toString().trim();
            if (v.length() > 70) v = v.substring(0, 70) + "...";
            return v;
        } catch (Exception e) {
            return null;
        }
    }

    private static boolean crtNoise(String n) {
        return n.startsWith("_") || n.startsWith("$") || n.contains("ftol") || n.contains("_CI");
    }

    @Override
    public void run() throws Exception {
        String[] args = getScriptArgs();
        int maxFn = (args.length > 0) ? Integer.parseInt(args[0]) : 15;
        long minBytes = (args.length > 1) ? Long.parseLong(args[1]) : 1500L;

        // pick candidates: internal-only, >= minBytes, deepest-first by size
        List<String[]> cand = new ArrayList<>();
        File csv = new File(OUT + "calltree_nodes.csv");
        try (BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(new FileInputStream(csv), "UTF-8"))) {
            String line = br.readLine();
            while ((line = br.readLine()) != null) {
                String[] p = line.split(",", -1);
                if (p.length < 6) continue;
                if (!p[5].isEmpty()) continue;              // has boundary callees -> skip
                if (Long.parseLong(p[3]) < minBytes) continue;
                cand.add(p);
            }
        } catch (Exception e) { println("ERROR reading " + csv + ": " + e.getMessage()); return; }
        cand.sort((a, b) -> Long.compare(Long.parseLong(b[3]), Long.parseLong(a[3])));
        if (cand.size() > maxFn) cand = cand.subList(0, maxFn);

        FunctionManager fm = currentProgram.getFunctionManager();
        Memory mem = currentProgram.getMemory();

        try (PrintWriter pw = new PrintWriter(new File(OUT + "annotation.csv"), "UTF-8")) {
            pw.println("address,name,bytes,subsystems,named_callees,strings");
            for (String[] row : cand) {
                Address a = currentProgram.getAddressFactory().getDefaultAddressSpace()
                        .getAddress(Long.parseLong(row[1], 16));
                Function f = fm.getFunctionAt(a);
                if (f == null) continue;

                Set<String> strs = new LinkedHashSet<>();
                Set<String> namedCals = new TreeSet<>();
                for (Address ia : f.getBody().getAddresses(true)) {
                    Instruction insn = getInstructionAt(ia);
                    if (insn == null) continue;
                    for (Reference r : insn.getReferencesFrom()) {
                        Address to = r.getToAddress();
                        if (to == null) continue;
                        if (!mem.contains(to)) continue;
                        if (insn.getFlowType().isCall()) {
                            Function t = fm.getFunctionAt(to);
                            if (t != null && !t.getName().startsWith("FUN_") && !crtNoise(t.getName())) namedCals.add(t.getName());
                        }
                        String s = stringAt(to);
                        if (s != null && s.length() > 2) strs.add(s);
                    }
                }
                pw.println(a + "," + f.getName() + "," + f.getBody().getNumAddresses() + "," + row[5] + "," +
                        String.join("|", namedCals) + "," + String.join("|", strs));
                println("ANNOTATE " + a + " " + f.getName() + " (" + f.getBody().getNumAddresses() + "B) " +
                        "calls=[" + String.join(",", namedCals) + "] " +
                        "strings=[" + String.join(" / ", strs.size() > 6 ? new ArrayList<>(strs).subList(0, 6) : strs) + "]");
            }
        }
    }
}

```

`port/ghidra/CallTree.java`:

```java
import ghidra.app.script.GhidraScript;
import ghidra.program.model.address.Address;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.Function;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.FunctionManager;

import java.io.BufferedReader;
import java.io.File;
import java.io.FileInputStream;
import java.io.InputStreamReader;
import java.io.PrintWriter;
import java.util.*;

/**
 * Walk the call graph down from WinMain_ and label each reachable function with the
 * subsystem(s) its direct callees belong to (graphics/audio/ui/os/video/assets/...),
 * using the symbol->subsystem map built from the SR-Septerra shim headers.
 *
 * Usage: -postScript CallTree.java [rootHex] [maxDepth]
 * Defaults: root 0x412b80 (WinMain_), depth 6.
 *
 * Output: work/ghidra-out/calltree_nodes.csv, calltree_edges.csv
 */
public class CallTree extends GhidraScript {

    private static final String OUT = "H:/ghidra/work/ghidra-out/";
    private static final String SYMBOLS = OUT + "shim_symbols.csv";

    private Map<String, String> subOf = new HashMap<>();   // symbol or base -> subsystem

    private static String base(String n) {
        for (String suf : new String[]{"_asm2c", "_c2asm", "_c"}) {
            if (n.endsWith(suf)) return n.substring(0, n.length() - suf.length());
        }
        return n;
    }

    private void loadSymbols() throws Exception {
        File f = new File(SYMBOLS);
        if (!f.exists()) { println("WARN: no symbol map at " + SYMBOLS); return; }
        try (BufferedReader br = new BufferedReader(new InputStreamReader(new FileInputStream(f), "UTF-8"))) {
            String line = br.readLine(); // header
            while ((line = br.readLine()) != null) {
                String[] p = line.split(",");
                if (p.length < 3) continue;
                subOf.put(p[0].trim(), p[2].trim());
                subOf.put(p[1].trim(), p[2].trim());
            }
        }
        println("loaded " + subOf.size() + " symbol->subsystem mappings");
    }

    private Set<String> subsystemsOf(Function f, Set<Function> callees) {
        Set<String> out = new TreeSet<>();
        for (Function c : callees) {
            String n = c.getName();
            String s = subOf.get(n);
            if (s == null) s = subOf.get(base(n));
            if (s != null) out.add(s);
        }
        return out;
    }

    @Override
    public void run() throws Exception {
        loadSymbols();

        String[] args = getScriptArgs();
        long rootAddr = (args.length > 0) ? Long.parseLong(args[0].replace("0x", ""), 16) : 0x412b80L;
        int maxDepth = (args.length > 1) ? Integer.parseInt(args[1]) : 6;

        Address ra = currentProgram.getAddressFactory().getDefaultAddressSpace().getAddress(rootAddr);
        FunctionManager fm = currentProgram.getFunctionManager();
        Function root = fm.getFunctionAt(ra);
        if (root == null) { println("ERROR: no function at " + ra); return; }
        println("root: " + root.getName() + " @ " + ra + " depth<=" + maxDepth);

        Map<Function, Integer> depthOf = new LinkedHashMap<>();
        Map<Function, Set<Function>> callees = new LinkedHashMap<>();
        ArrayDeque<Function> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
        depthOf.put(root, 0);
        queue.add(root);

        while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
            Function f = queue.poll();
            int d = depthOf.get(f);
            Set<Function> cs;
            try { cs = new TreeSet<>(Comparator.comparing(Function::getEntryPoint)); cs.addAll(f.getCalledFunctions(monitor)); }
            catch (Exception e) { cs = new TreeSet<>(); }
            callees.put(f, cs);
            if (d >= maxDepth) continue;
            for (Function c : cs) {
                if (!depthOf.containsKey(c)) { depthOf.put(c, d + 1); queue.add(c); }
            }
        }

        // edges + nodes
        new File(OUT).mkdirs();
        try (PrintWriter en = new PrintWriter(new File(OUT + "calltree_nodes.csv"), "UTF-8");
             PrintWriter ee = new PrintWriter(new File(OUT + "calltree_edges.csv"), "UTF-8")) {
            en.println("depth,address,name,bytes,callees,subsystems");
            ee.println("caller_address,caller_name,callee_address,callee_name");
            for (Map.Entry<Function, Integer> e : depthOf.entrySet()) {
                Function f = e.getKey();
                Set<Function> cs = callees.getOrDefault(f, Collections.emptySet());
                Set<String> subs = subsystemsOf(f, cs);
                en.println(e.getValue() + "," + f.getEntryPoint() + "," + f.getName() + "," +
                        f.getBody().getNumAddresses() + "," + cs.size() + "," + String.join(";", subs));
                for (Function c : cs) {
                    ee.println(f.getEntryPoint() + "," + f.getName() + "," +
                            c.getEntryPoint() + "," + c.getName());
                }
            }
        }

        // summary
        Map<Integer, Integer> byDepth = new TreeMap<>();
        Map<String, Integer> bySub = new TreeMap<>();
        int pure = 0, total = depthOf.size();
        long bytes = 0;
        for (Map.Entry<Function, Integer> e : depthOf.entrySet()) {
            byDepth.merge(e.getValue(), 1, Integer::sum);
            bytes += e.getKey().getBody().getNumAddresses();
            Set<String> subs = subsystemsOf(e.getKey(), callees.getOrDefault(e.getKey(), Collections.emptySet()));
            if (subs.isEmpty()) pure++;
            for (String s : subs) bySub.merge(s, 1, Integer::sum);
        }
        println("CALLTREE reachable=" + total + " bytes=" + bytes + " internal_only=" + pure);
        println("CALLTREE by depth: " + byDepth);
        println("CALLTREE functions calling into each subsystem: " + bySub);

        int all = fm.getFunctionCount();
        println("CALLTREE uncovered_by_walk=" + (all - total) + " of " + all + " total functions");
    }
}

```

`port/ghidra/Decompile.java`:

```java
import ghidra.app.decompiler.DecompInterface;
import ghidra.app.decompiler.DecompileResults;
import ghidra.app.script.GhidraScript;
import ghidra.program.model.address.Address;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.Function;
import ghidra.program.model.listing.FunctionManager;

import java.io.File;
import java.io.PrintWriter;
import java.util.LinkedHashSet;
import java.util.Set;
import java.util.TreeSet;
import java.util.regex.Matcher;
import java.util.regex.Pattern;

/**
 * Decompile one function and report readability metrics.
 *
 * Usage: -postScript Decompile.java 0x430500 [timeoutSeconds]
 * Writes work/ghidra-out/decomp_<addr>.c and prints metrics.
 */
public class Decompile extends GhidraScript {

    private static final String OUT = "H:/ghidra/work/ghidra-out/";

    private static int count(String hay, String needle) {
        int n = 0, i = 0;
        while ((i = hay.indexOf(needle, i)) >= 0) { n++; i += needle.length(); }
        return n;
    }

    private static int countRegex(String hay, String re) {
        Matcher m = Pattern.compile(re).matcher(hay);
        int n = 0;
        while (m.find()) n++;
        return n;
    }

    @Override
    public void run() throws Exception {
        java.util.List<String> specs = new java.util.ArrayList<>();
        for (String a0 : getScriptArgs()) for (String piece : a0.split(","))
            if (!piece.trim().isEmpty()) specs.add(piece.trim());
        if (specs.isEmpty()) specs.add("0x430500");
        for (String spec : specs) decompileOne(Long.parseLong(spec.replace("0x", ""), 16));
    }

    private void decompileOne(long addr) throws Exception {
        int timeout = 180;

        Address a = currentProgram.getAddressFactory().getDefaultAddressSpace().getAddress(addr);
        FunctionManager fm = currentProgram.getFunctionManager();
        Function f = fm.getFunctionAt(a);
        if (f == null) { println("ERROR: no function at " + a); return; }

        DecompInterface di = new DecompInterface();
        di.toggleCCode(true);
        di.toggleSyntaxTree(true);
        di.openProgram(currentProgram);
        DecompileResults res = di.decompileFunction(f, timeout, monitor);
        if (!res.decompileCompleted()) {
            println("DECOMP FAILED: " + res.getErrorMessage());
            return;
        }
        String c = res.getDecompiledFunction().getC();

        new File(OUT).mkdirs();
        File out = new File(OUT + "decomp_" + Long.toHexString(addr) + ".c");
        try (PrintWriter pw = new PrintWriter(out, "UTF-8")) { pw.print(c); }

        // metrics
        Set<String> globals = new TreeSet<>();
        Matcher m = Pattern.compile("\\b(?:DAT|UNK|PTR|BYTE|WORD|DWORD|QWORD|LAB|FLOAT|DOUBLE)_([0-9a-fA-F]{5,8})\\b")
                .matcher(c);
        while (m.find()) globals.add(m.group(0));

        Set<String> callees = new TreeSet<>();
        Matcher m2 = Pattern.compile("\\b([A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*)\\s*\\(").matcher(c);
        while (m2.find()) {
            String n = m2.group(1);
            if (!n.equals("if") && !n.equals("while") && !n.equals("for") && !n.equals("switch")
                    && !n.equals("return") && !n.equals("sizeof") && !n.equals("do")) callees.add(n);
        }

        println("DECOMP " + a + " " + f.getName() + " file=" + out.getAbsolutePath());
        println("DECOMP lines=" + c.split("\n").length +
                " bytes=" + c.length());
        println("DECOMP loops: while=" + count(c, "while (") + " do=" + count(c, "do {") +
                " for=" + count(c, "for (") +
                " | switch=" + count(c, "switch (") + " case=" + count(c, "case "));
        println("DECOMP ifs=" + count(c, "if (") + " else=" + count(c, "else") +
                " gotos=" + count(c, "goto ") + " labels=" + countRegex(c, "(?m)^\\s*LAB_[0-9a-fA-F]+:"));
        println("DECOMP globals_touched=" + globals.size() + " distinct_callees=" + callees.size());
        println("DECOMP globals_sample=" + new java.util.ArrayList<>(globals).subList(0, Math.min(12, globals.size())));
        println("DECOMP callees_sample=" + new java.util.ArrayList<>(callees).subList(0, Math.min(12, callees.size())));
        println("DECOMP unaff_noise=" + count(c, "unaff_") + " instack_noise=" + count(c, "in_stack_"));
    }
}
